2026-09-29 | slo-offer, slo-roadmap-widget | THE $297 CARD IS TYPED ON /roadmap NOW, NOT ON A COMMAS PAGE (owner-set 2026-09-29). Step 2 draws Commas' own card form inside the widget and charges in place; nobody is sent to a hosted checkout and there is no return trip to wait for. POST /api/public/slo-checkout mints an embedded checkout session on the SAME Commas session the hosted mint already made under the keep title "Consulting Services Assessment", and answers a new `embedded` block (creatorId, productId, sessionSecret, environment). No catalog product is created and the amount still follows the order. MEASURED AGAINST THE WHOLE VENDOR API, because the ask was to post the card to our own server: Commas has no endpoint that accepts a card number. The only routes that take money from a new buyer are the hosted payment_link and this embedded session; POST /public-api/customers/:id/charge re-bills a card Commas already holds and cannot accept one being typed for the first time. So the card boxes had to be theirs, and no card number reaches a Fundhub server. TWO DEFECTS DIED WITH THE OLD STEP 2. (1) The four card inputs on the page were decoys — they collected a card number, cleared it, and sent the buyer to a Commas page to type it a second time. (2) Their expiry check reused the business start-date parser, which demands MM/YYYY, while the box said MM/YY and the error said "Use MM/YY, like 09/28" — so a correct 09/28 was refused and nobody could get past step 2. AND THE KEY WAS DEAD: the value stored as FANBASIS_CHECKOUT_API_KEY answers 401 on every Commas route, which is why a live attempt saved the contact and never returned a card page at all. checkoutConfig now reads CORTANA_COMMAS_API_KEY first, which answers 200 for the same seller (handle fundhub-1, the keep catalog); the broken value is left exactly where it is. public/roadmap/pay.html is untouched and still navigates to checkoutUrl, which is still answered and still written to payment_links because the payment webhook is matched to that row. | A buyer typed a card into boxes that threw it away, was bounced to Commas to type it again, and before that the expiry check refused a valid date on a checkout whose API key was returning 401 | (this commit)

MEASURED WHILE DOING IT, reported not fixed: an extra business picked on step 3 is NOT charged — runSloPull records it as slo_business_owed_cents and its own header says nothing there can charge a card; the only charge path in the repo is createCheckoutSession, which mints a hosted Commas card page, and its three types (onetime_non_reusable, onetime_reusable, subscription) include nothing that charges a card already on file, which is why slo-repair-checkout sends buyers to a second card page. A literal one-click upsell cannot be built from what is here today. Also measured: outbound SMS is gated on opt-OUT (src/messaging/gate.mjs isOptedOut), not on a positive consent row, so this change adds the record, it does not unblock sending that was blocked. | Asking a stranger for their EIN and NAICS code before taking a dollar, and texting people whose agreement never mentioned texts | (this commit)

2026-09-24 | ad-video | Two more breaks found by the same first take, both between files. (1) pipeline.mjs called naming.rawName/adFolderName/briefName/finalName; naming.mjs exports rawFileName/paulFolderName/briefFileName/finalFileName - different names and argument shapes, and pipeline.test.mjs passed on a stub that invented the first set. The rename guard was simply false (Drive file kept its phone name) and delivery would have waited for ever on 'the naming module was not supplied'. Pipeline now calls the real functions; seam.test.mjs checks every naming.* call against the module's exports. (2) Submagic hands back a user-media id before it has finished taking the clip in; placing it a second later is refused 'media is not ready yet'. The export then went out with zero clips - a billed render, empty. The placement is now asked again on a clock (~90 s) and a take WAITS rather than exporting an empty cut over a transient refusal; a refusal for a real reason still exports captions-only with the reason on the row. 2026-09-24 | ad-video | The first real take ran end to end through the match step, and the run found the pipeline's two lists of states disagreeing, plus five more breaks. (1) states.mjs TRANSITIONS said staged -> transcribed -> matched -> editing; the pipeline (and this journey's own diagram) goes staged -> editing -> transcribed -> matched. The upload succeeded, Submagic returned a project id, and the pipeline threw 'cannot go staged -> editing' writing it down - a paid project lost. Corrected; transitions-match-the-pipeline.test.mjs pins the two together. (2) A script had nowhere to carry its ad number: match.mjs asks for candidates with adId, candidateScripts() selected none, ad_scripts had no column. Migration 393 adds ad_scripts.ad_id; only scripts with a number are offered. (3) The seven locked $297 ads were not in ad_scripts at all - the matcher was handed one unrelated walkthrough and correctly refused. scripts/ad-scripts-load-locked.mjs loads them verbatim from docs/ads/fundhub-297/FundHub-LOCKED-ADS.md as ad numbers 84-90 (agent-set: next free above the registry's 83; 1-7 avoided so historical utm_content clicks cannot attach). (4) A row that already holds a project id now RESUMES into editing instead of skipping in place, and a row with its transcript resumes into transcribed - a retry must not buy a second project. Four paid projects for one take were measured in the account tonight. (5) The take number is read from the phone's file name ('Take 2') instead of being left NULL. (6) transcript_words is jsonb and was handed a raw array - JSON.stringify first. (7) An expired spend claim now fails the take with the reason on the row instead of spending again. GAP, not reconciled: retryFailed() still clears submagic_project_id (this journey, 2026-09-22 entry, on purpose), so a failed -> staged retry re-creates a project and pays again even when the existing one is healthy. Left for an owner call. No state added, removed or renamed. | The pipeline had never run end to end; every step's first real execution was tonight | (this commit)

2026-09-22 | ad-video | Four defects fixed and the pipeline's folders and settings put in place. (1) THE MARK NOW GOES DOWN BEFORE THE MONEY GOES OUT. The two steps that cost money - starting a Submagic project and asking for the render - wrote their 'this already ran' mark only AFTER the vendor answered. An upload of a 200 MB take runs for minutes, and a worker killed in that window came back to a row saying nothing had happened, so it paid again. New columns submagic_claimed_at and export_claimed_at (db/migrations/391_ad_video_spend_claims.sql) are written first, through a new claim port the sweeper supplies, and cleared the moment the vendor answers. No claim written means no call made. A standing create claim refuses a second create, because Submagic publishes no way to list projects and nothing can find out whether the first one landed; a standing export claim polls instead, which costs nothing and answers it outright. (2) A RETRIED TAKE USED TO STALL FOR EVER. retryFailed() put the row back at 'staged', but every later step skips when its own mark is set and a skip writes nothing - so the status never moved again, silently, on every five-minute pass. It now clears the project id, transcript, rename, b-roll, export, render, notification and both claims; the inputs (Drive file, ad number, take number, matched script) survive. (3) THE 'link' STAGING MODE IS DELETED. It shared one take as 'anyone with the link, reader' so Submagic could fetch it, and nothing ever took that share back off - and Google grants no expiry on an 'anyone' permission, so there was no small fix. shareAnyoneWithLink() is gone from the Drive provider too, so nothing in this repo can publish a Drive file at all. Submagic takes the bytes directly, so the route was never needed. (4) B-ROLL WAS POSTING TO A GUESSED PATH. It now uses the one route Submagic documents, POST /v1/user-media/upload, multipart with the clip's bytes in a 'file' field. Also: a Raw folder created inside SLO Ads for takes to land in, the existing paul-submagic folder reused for finished work, and DRIVE_RAW_FOLDER_ID, DRIVE_PAUL_FOLDER_ID, SUBMAGIC_WEBHOOK_URL and NTFY_TOPIC (--secret) set on Netlify for production, deploy-preview and branch-deploy. No state added, removed or renamed. No Submagic call of any kind was made and no project was created. pg tests NOT RUN - no local Postgres, so none of the SQL has been executed. | A crash mid-upload could be billed twice, a retry never moved, and a take stayed world-readable for ever | (this commit)
2026-09-22 | ad-video | The pipeline can now actually move a take. Three missing pieces landed. (1) STAGING: src/ad-videos/staging.mjs. The old note said a Drive link cannot work and the take would have to be copied to a new host (Cloudflare R2 or Amazon S3) first - measured false on both counts. Submagic takes the file itself (POST /v1/projects/upload, up to 2 GB), and a Drive link works anyway (a 344.6 MB file served real video to a client with no sign-in, using confirm=t). Default mode is 'direct': no link is made, nothing is shared, and the bytes go Drive -> worker -> Submagic. AD_VIDEO_STAGING_MODE=link is the fallback and shares ONE file read-only by its unguessable id; a typo in that variable reads as direct, so a misspelling can never make a video public. Netlify Blobs (no public URL, 20 MB function response) and Supabase Storage (50 MiB file cap on our plan) were both measured and both refused. (2) BINARY TRANSFER: transmitBinary() and postBinaryTo() inside src/lib/outbound-fetch.mjs. Video bytes could not move at all before, because the one outbound door read every response as text. The new path is INSIDE the same fence - a caller still names a fence, the dry-run flags still hold it, and src/lib/no-unfenced-transmit.test.mjs is untouched and green - with a size cap that bites while reading (a vendor that lies about content-length cannot fill the worker's memory) and a two-minute clock. (3) DRIVE WRITE: downloadFile(), shareAnyoneWithLink() and a real uploadVideo() (resumable, two calls). uploadVideo used to refuse; the finished ad now lands in Paul's folder. The live token was measured to hold the full drive scope, so nothing about any stored credential changed. Also corrected from the same measurement: b-roll was posting to POST /v1/projects/{id}/user-media, which is a 404 and not an auth problem - it is POST /v1/user-media; and creates are 30 an hour, not the 500 an earlier note recorded, so create is now the tightest limit on the plan, not export. store.patch() can finally write the fourteen worker marks migration 390 added - it was throwing on every one of them, and one throw ended a whole sweeper pass. No state added, removed or renamed. pg tests NOT RUN - no local Postgres, so none of the SQL has been executed. | The pipeline stopped dead at the first step, on a research note that turned out to be wrong | (this commit)
2026-09-22 | ad-video | Merge review: the workers and the table now agree. Migration 390 adds the fourteen marks each worker step leaves behind - exported_at among them, the one that stops a take being re-exported every five minutes at Submagic's per-minute rate - and makes ad_id and take_no nullable until the transcript match names them, with a new check that demands both from 'matched' onward. Three columns renamed to the table's own names. The store gained the six functions the sweeper had been probing for and not finding, so the sweeper stopped reporting 'the store does not offer listPending/patch' on every pass and doing nothing. The Submagic webhook now finds its row (it was passing a pool where a staff transaction belongs, so row-level security silently matched nothing and every render was dropped). Chris's phone notification now carries real Approve and Reject links, minted per row just before the buzz. New guard src/ad-videos/seam.test.mjs, no database needed. pg tests NOT RUN - no local Postgres, so none of the SQL has been executed. | Three agents each finished green and the pipeline still could not move a video: every defect was in the gap between two files no test loaded together | (this commit)

The door is GET/POST /api/public/ad-video-approve, whose token lives on the ad_videos row itself (migration 389) and whose row-level security policies are written on that token, so a request with no live token matches zero rows. A GET now renders a real page with Approve and Reject buttons (src/ad-videos/decision-page.mjs, kept from the duplicate) instead of raw JSON, because a phone notification is opened by a browser; only a POST decides, so a link preview or a URL scanner cannot approve anything. Migration 390 and its second token table were removed with the door they served, and the second copy of the ntfy provider went with them. 2026-09-22 | client, role-owner, role-sales-manager, role-closer, role-funding-advisor, role-inquiry-remover, affiliate, white-label (regenerated; two new routes) | The ad video pipeline gets its record. A new table holds one row per filmed TAKE of one ad number, and a take is never overwritten — a re-film is a new row at the next take number, so a rejected take keeps its reason forever. The database itself now refuses a second finished video for one ad number, refuses an ad number written with padding (043 and 43 would be two different ads to fundhub_ad_id, splitting one ad's results in half), refuses a failure or a rejection with no reason, and refuses to approve a video that is not an ad unless its real height is 4K (.claude/rules/video-4k-unless-ad.md). Two new routes: GET /api/ad-videos, the queue of takes and where each one is stuck (owner and admin only — these rows carry unreleased ad creative and links to the raw files), and the open door GET/POST /api/public/ad-video-approve, where Chris taps Approve or Reject from a phone notification. That door has no sign-in because a notification has none: the random token in the link is the credential, it reaches exactly one take through the table's own security rules, it expires, and using it spends it. No screen built yet and nothing approves automatically — only a person can approve. States and diagram: docs/video-pipeline-plan.md §2 and §5. | Owner, 2026-09-22: one ad number = one finished video, Chris only films and approves | (this commit)

2026-09-17 | doc-check-identity-flow | THE DOCUMENT READER NOW SURVIVES A DRY AI ACCOUNT. Measured on production the same day: twelve DOC-CHECK runs, every one `openai 429 - You have no credits remaining`, every one recorded as a finished run with nothing holding a note to come back. Funding the account would not have unstuck any of them, because with no new upload there is no docs.received and so no reader - and dispute letters cannot stage until an ID has been read. Now: classifyModelFailure() in src/agents/model.mjs tells a temporary failure (no credit, rate limit, vendor 5xx, unreachable) from a permanent one, and callOpenAI/callAnthropic carry the HTTP status out with the error instead of only embedding it in a string. src/handlers/doc-check.mjs parks a temporary failure on failed_events (039) as pending with a next_attempt_at, twelve attempts rather than the default seven so the backoff reaches about nine days, and raises a task that says it is WAITING rather than one that says check it by hand. New src/workflows/doc-check-retry-sweeper.mjs re-reads the due ones every 20 minutes and resolves, reschedules or exhausts each row; it claims ONLY rows whose handler is doc-check, because failed_events holds 28 pending money and survey rows that nothing has ever retried and draining those is a different decision. A retry records its own agent_runs row (the event unique index would otherwise drop it, leaving '429' as the file's last word forever). NOTHING GUESSES AN IDENTITY: a document still unread leaves verified_legal_name, verified_address and verified_date_of_birth exactly as they were - no placeholder, nothing off the credit report. No new dependency, no model or provider change. RUN HERE: node --test on doc-check.test.mjs + doc-check-retry-sweeper.test.mjs + model.test.mjs = 39 pass 0 fail 0 skipped; index.test.mjs + registry.test.mjs = 21 pass 0 fail. NOT RUN, and not pretended otherwise: the full suite, lint and tsc were not run (five agents share this checkout), no Playwright check (no UI changed), and no sweep has ever executed against the live database - the first real proof is the first tick after this ships. | An empty wallet on one afternoon froze credit repair for every client who uploaded during it, permanently, and the only way back was a person finding each file and asking for it again | uncommitted

2026-09-09 | ad-label-spine | THE SYNC ONLY EVER READ THE FIRST PAGE OF EVERY LIST META SENDS, AND SAID NOTHING. Meta answers a list request with a page of rows plus a link to the next page. api/campaigns/sync.mjs asked for campaigns, ad sets and ads with limit=100 and never once followed that link, so an ad account with more than 100 campaigns, or an ad set with more than 100 ads, silently lost everything past the first hundred — every run, forever, with no error and nothing on screen. The Campaign Manager just showed fewer ads than exist. FIXED BY REUSING WHAT THIS REPO ALREADY HAD (CLAUDE.md section 8): the insights pager was already correct, so it was renamed fetchAllPages() and all four lists now go through it — the numbers, the campaigns, the ad sets and the ads. The old name fetchInsightPages stays as an alias so the tests and journey pages that name it keep working. metaListUrl() builds the first page with the same fields and the same limit=100 the single-page read used, so nothing about the request changed except that the answer is now followed to its end. THE CAP IS 50 PAGES OF 100 = 5,000 ROWS PER LIST, chosen because Meta's own published per-ad-account ceilings are 5,000 campaigns, 5,000 ad sets and 5,000 ads, so the cap is a stop and not a limit a real account meets. HITTING IT IS SAID OUT LOUD, which is half the fix: listTruncationMessage() puts a line into the run's errors, which makes buildSyncResponse() answer ok:false with partial:true, so the screen prints "stopped after 50 pages of ads — the list was cut short, so some ads are missing" instead of a green tick over a short list. Replacing one silent truncation with another would have been no fix. NEW TEST src/http/campaigns-sync-paging.test.mjs, 15 tests, under src/ so npm test's glob picks it up (CLAUDE.md section 12): it drives a fake Meta that hands out a second and third page and proves the rows past page one arrive, that the next-page link Meta itself sent is the URL actually requested, that a list that never ends is stopped at the cap, that the rows already read are kept rather than thrown away, that truncated comes back true, and that a cut-short list reaches the answer the screen reads. It passes 15/15 here. NOT PROVEN: no Meta ad account is connected, so no page of real campaigns has ever been walked; every .pg.test.mjs covering this chain still skips because there is no Postgres on this machine, so campaigns-sync-activation and campaigns-video-metrics have never run. No Playwright check was run. | a silent loss of rows that can never be recovered later is the worst failure this file can have, and the repo already had the right shape for it in src/analytics/clickfunnels.mjs:162-174 | uncommitted

2026-09-09 | ad-label-spine | THE MOMENT META ANSWERS IS NOW THE MOMENT THE AD ACCOUNT IS SWITCHED ON, AND THE SECOND SWITCH FINALLY HAS A HAND ON IT. Two dead ends removed from api/campaigns/sync.mjs. (1) THE SWITCH-ON MOVED UP. The line that promotes a connection from 'pending' to 'active' sat BELOW the walk over every campaign, every ad set and every ad. That walk is one call per campaign and one per ad set, which this file's own header says a big account cannot finish inside the time limit — so on a busy account the promotion was never reached, the row stayed 'pending', and the next press did exactly the same thing. The same permanent dead end, just moved to bigger accounts. The UPDATE now runs the instant the campaign list comes back, before the walk and before any further call to Meta. Nothing else about it changed: Meta answering a read is still the only thing that earns 'active'; a failure still leaves the row pending with Meta's own sentence in last_error. Proof arriving is what is recorded, and everything after the two account-level reads (the insights pull and the campaign list) is OUR writing, not proof of access. (2) platform_verification_state IS NOW WRITABLE. db/migrations/046_ad_platforms.sql:414-418 refuses to put a credit-related campaign live unless that column says 'approved', and NOTHING in the repository ever wrote 'approved' — api/campaigns/meta-agency.mjs:169 inserts 'unverified' and there was no other writer, so 'go live' would have failed with a database error forever. Campaigns really do go live from here (api/campaigns/write.mjs:112), so the gate really did bite. The sync now reads Meta's own verification_status back off the Business node and writes what Meta said: 'verified' becomes approved, anything pending becomes submitted, a refusal becomes rejected, and an unknown word or no answer at all writes NOTHING and leaves the column exactly as it was. WE NEVER AWARD 'approved' OURSELVES. That read is never fatal and is never counted as a failure of the run — a token without business_management scope costs nothing and the badge simply keeps saying unverified, which is true. NO NEW PAGE, TAB, MENU ROW, ROUTE OR DEPENDENCY; no journey diagram changed because no route changed. There is no such field, in any spelling — checked 2026-09-09 against Meta's own Python SDK field list, facebook_business/adobjects/adsinsights.py. WHY THAT WAS NOT A SMALL BUG: Meta refuses the ENTIRE insights request when one field name is unknown; it does not skip the bad name and answer the rest. So that one invented word would have emptied spend, clicks and impressions too, and the first time Chris connected a Meta ad account the numbers would have come back blank with nothing saying why. THE FIX: the field is now video_continuous_2_sec_watched_actions — the real field closest in meaning, people who kept watching past the opening — stored in a column named for what it holds, video_continuous_2s_watched, NOT for 3 seconds. video_play_actions was added alongside it (column video_plays, how many plays started at all), free on the same request. Eight video fields now, not seven. The other six were already correct and were not touched. MIGRATION 378 WAS EDITED IN PLACE, WHICH IS NORMALLY FORBIDDEN. CLAUDE.md section 12 still stands: editing an applied migration is a silent no-op. 378 has never been applied anywhere — it was written today, the live database is at 270 migrations, and there is no other environment — so no database has ever had the old column and a superseding migration would have renamed something that never existed. 378's header now says this, and says to write 379 instead if anyone reads it after 378 has run. HOOK RATE AND HOLD RATE NOW SAY WHAT THEY ARE. watchRate() at src/ops/meta-marketing.mjs is unchanged arithmetic; its comment, and the ones in api/read/ad-spine.mjs, now state plainly that Meta publishes no 3-second field, so this is NOT the number Ads Manager prints beside the words 'hook rate' and must not be labelled as if it were. Hook rate is people who kept watching past the opening over impressions. RECORDED, NOT BUILT: video_play_curve_actions returns a per-second retention curve for an ad on this same call for free — where people actually leave, second by second. Better than the five percentage points. Written into 378's header and meta.mjs as the next step; it is a list per row, not one number, so it needs its own shape. THE WHOLE POINT OF IT, AND THE THING MOST EASILY THROWN AWAY: A DASH IS NOT A ZERO AND THEY DO NOT LOOK ALIKE. GET /api/read/ad-spine in grouped mode (?group_by=angle|hook|lane|offer|script_type&days=1..365, default 30) now returns, per label: spend_cents, impressions, clicks, ad_days_reported, people, people_booked and cost_per_booked_person — the two joins 377's own header named and neither of which existed (ad_metrics_daily on ad_row_id; client_ad_attribution on fundhub_ad_number). THE LIST MODE IS BYTE-FOR-BYTE UNCHANGED: no window, no money, window:null in the body. FOUR DECISIONS WORTH THE RECORD. (1) NULL SURVIVES: the spend join is LEFT and is never coalesced, so a label with no ad_metrics_daily row at all returns spend_cents null, not 0 — 'nobody told us' and 'we spent nothing' are different facts (CLAUDE.md section 12). ad_days_reported says how many ad-days the sum came from. (2) THE SMALL-SAMPLE RULE IS REUSED, NOT REWRITTEN: cost_per_booked_person is costPerBooked() from src/ops/meta-marketing.mjs:17, which refuses to divide under MIN_N_RATE (10, src/ops/discoveries.mjs:9) and returns status INSUFFICIENT with a null cost and a plain-words reason. No second threshold was invented. (3) THE LEADING-ZERO TRAP IS HANDLED IN ONE PLACE: fundhub_ad_id() keeps leading zeros so utm_content=042 stores '042', and a human typing 42 into ads.fundhub_ad_number would never have matched it — silently, with no error. AD_NUMBER_MATCH (api/read/ad-spine.mjs:171) compares the two as bigints, which cannot raise because both columns carry the same one-to-nine-digit CHECK (377:569, 286:116-117). Cost of that choice, stated on the journey page: two ads numbered '042' and '42' are now treated as one. (4) PEOPLE, NOT CALLS: the booking test is exactly the one src/ads/store.mjs:68-70 already uses (a bookings row, same client and org, status not 'cancelled') but counted as count(DISTINCT client_id) instead of count(b.id), so a person who rebooks counts once — that is why the field is people_booked and why the older rollup's rate can read over 100%. The money and people totals are computed in two separate passes joined on the label key, because joining a per-day table and a per-person table to the same rows multiplies each by the other; ads is count(DISTINCT ad_row_id) for the same reason. A group in which no ad carries our number returns people null rather than 0, because nobody could be matched even in principle; ads_with_number says how many could. The credit-limit cell holds one of THREE things — a positive number, the number ZERO, or nothing at all — and the code asked only "is it null?". A limit reported as $0 is a KNOWN value, ten percent of it is $0, and that was printed as an instruction: "Pay SECURED CARD from $900 down to $0" in credit_analysis_report.html, funding_snapshot.html and optimization_roadmap.html, and in all four bodies of the WeasyPrint printer, three lines under the same card's own row which correctly printed dashes. Reproduced 2026-09-06 through the real mapper on one open card, SECURED CARD, balance 900, effectiveLimit 0, engine utilization {totalBalance:900,totalLimit:0,pct:null}. A reported $0 and an unreported limit share the OUTCOME — no target — and not the WORDS: telling the holder of a card whose limit IS reported, as $0, that "no credit limit is reported for this card" is its own false sentence. The three printers now say "The credit limit reported for this card is $0" and "No credit limit is reported for this card" respectively, and "No open card on this file reports a credit limit above $0" for the total, which is true in both states. The mapper, the waypoint checklist (src/waypoints/definitions.mjs:235,324) and the vendor engine (vendor/underwriteiq-crs/optimization-findings.js:115) were each opened and already guard on `> 0`; nothing else in the tree runs a reported zero through the 10% arithmetic. Both reviewers' measurement reproduced here before the fix, on a scratch Postgres 16.14: 200 clients each holding an unpaid checkout minted 400 days ago against a checklist row 400 days late, plus one newly late live client -> candidates 200, the live one not among them, 0 messages to them, budget full, and the same a year later; 0 ladder rows ever written for those 200, which is why round two's anti-join could not see them. The number lives in one file (src/paid-services/link-ttl.mjs), it is stamped on the record when the link is made, db/migrations/370 REFUSES a record sitting in 'waiting for payment' without one, an hourly job (src/workflows/paid-checkout-expiry-sweeper.mjs) closes the record to 'cancelled' with reason 'checkout_expired' when the date passes, and the chase search holds a place open only while the link is genuinely live. Seven and not thirty because the whole ladder is nine days long; a longer hold would swallow it. NO MONEY MOVES — a hosted link is an invitation and nothing has been charged, so closing one takes nothing from anybody and creates no refund, and the sweep refuses to touch any record that carries a payment amount or a payment time. Proved at 200 days and at 400 days: the 200 abandoned records are real work again, one pass hands each of them to a person and finishes their ladder for good, and THE LIVE CLIENT GETS THEIR MESSAGE ON THE VERY NEXT PASS — one hour, rather than never. (2) THE PERMANENCE PROMISE WAS STILL NOT TRUE. Round three's take-away of delete and edit rights was real and both reviewers confirmed it, but 368 also said the escalation record is swept away automatically when the CLIENT record is deleted, and that sweep runs with the table owner's permission rather than the application's. The app holds delete on clients. So the app could still destroy the record, by deleting the client, while two more places said flatly "the application really cannot delete one". db/migrations/370 changes that link so the database REFUSES to delete a client who has one of these records on file. Proved as fundhub_app (not a superuser, does not bypass row-level security): the delete is rejected outright, error 23503, the record is still there afterwards, and a client with no such record still deletes normally. "that collection agency that keeps calling me is a scam" is a client complaining about a third party — the exact case the word list says it is trying to avoid — and it wrote the permanent record and ended every chase that client will ever have. Round three's take-away means no part of the app can undo it and nothing outside the chase code reads that table, so NO SCREEN SHOWED IT HAD HAPPENED. The stop stays permanent and the word list was NOT narrowed — every narrowing tried also let through language plainly aimed at us. 2026-09-06 | waypoint-nudge | ROUND THREE. THE LINE BELOW THIS ONE OVERCLAIMED AND THIS ONE CORRECTS IT — two of the four things it said were fixed were not. (1) THE CHASE STILL DIED FOR EVERYONE, AND THE ROUND-TWO FIX WAS AIMED AT THE WRONG CASE. The pass takes the 200 oldest late rows and only then asks, one at a time, whether each may be chased. Round two excluded a row whose current rung had ALREADY BEEN USED. The case that was actually failing writes no row at all: a client who said STOP, mentioned a lawyer, finished or cancelled their program, paid us to do it, or already replied is refused AFTER the 200 are chosen, nothing is written, so the row stays late, stays oldest, sorts FIRST and holds a place for ever. The sweeper searches every company at once, so those 200 places are one budget for the whole platform. Both reviewers reproduced it independently and both were reproduced again here before the fix: 200 cancelled programs plus one newly late client gave 200 candidates, the live one not among them, 0 messages, same again the next day; 200 clients who had texted STOP gave exactly the same. FIXED by moving every PERMANENT reason to stop INSIDE the query, before the limit — opted out, a lawyer, program complete or cancelled, paid the alternative, already replied, ladder finished, the row blocked, done, or ours rather than theirs. TEMPORARY reasons are deliberately left in the queue and keep their place, because tomorrow they can send: a checkout link still out, the one-message-a-day cap, night in the client's timezone, or a database error we could not read. This is a SHORTER queue and never a looser one — every clause mirrors a check the send-time gate was already making a moment later, the gate still runs on every message, and a test fails if the two ever disagree. Proved with both reviewers' scenarios and with a temporarily held row that keeps its place and is reached the day the hold lifts. One honest gap: a client who has threatened us but has never been scanned still holds a place for exactly ONE pass — the pass that scans them, refuses them and writes the permanent record; nothing is sent to them on it. (2) THE PERMANENCE PROMISE WAS FALSE, IN FIVE PLACES INCLUDING THE LINE BELOW. It said the application cannot delete a legal-escalation record. It could. db/migrations/104 runs ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES granting SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on every table made after it, so 368's GRANT SELECT, INSERT added nothing and removed nothing; confirmed on a migrated database, DELETE privilege = true. 368 now carries an explicit REVOKE of UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE — the shape the sister lane proved in 361 and 363 — and it is proved as fundhub_app (not a superuser, does not bypass row-level security) on a database built from zero: DELETE and UPDATE both raise permission denied, error 42501, and the row is still there afterwards; INSERT and SELECT still work, which the detector needs. A silent 'DELETE 0' would not have been a refusal. src/nudge/escalation-permanence.pg.test.mjs fails if any of that is ever undone. (3) THE LEGAL THREAT STILL EXPIRED. The line below says the read watermark advanced only over rows the pre-filter returned. The code advanced it to max(created_at) over ALL inbound rows and the next pass read with a strict 'created_at > mark', so a threat landing exactly ON the mark was invisible for ever. Reproduced here: scan 1 false, the lawyer row present in messages, scan 2 false, blockersFor [], deliverNudge queued, 1 message to the lawyer-threat client. A '>=' boundary would close that one shape and leave a backfilled, imported or clock-skewed message behind the mark permanently invisible, and `messages` has no insertion-ordered column to mark instead. SO THE WATERMARK IS GONE, table and all: the whole inbound history is read every pass until the permanent record exists. The cost was measured, not assumed — 3.5 ms at 500 inbound rows, 8.4 ms at 2,000, 39.9 ms at 10,000, and only ever for a client who has NOT threatened us. (4) THE JOURNEY PAGE DESCRIBED A SCREEN THAT DOES NOT EXIST. It said a page renders 'sent' as sent and 'filed' as filed alongside who said so. Nothing outside src/nudge/ reads regulator_complaints and src/nudge/regulator.mjs has no production caller — no endpoint, no route, no workflow. The page now says UNVERIFIED and says why. THE PRODUCT RULES ARE UNCHANGED: rounds 4 and 5 still claim no filing, no client-facing copy was touched, and 'credit repair' still appears in none of it. 2026-09-06 | waypoint-nudge | FOUR REAL DEFECTS IN THE OVERDUE CHASE, CLOSED AND PROVED. (1) THE CHASE HAD STOPPED WORKING FOR THE WHOLE PLATFORM AND LOOKED HEALTHY DOING IT. planNudges took the 200 oldest late rows and only then asked whether each still had a rung left, so a row whose four rungs were spent stayed late for ever and — being oldest — sorted FIRST, until finished rows held all 200 places. The sweeper searches every company at once, so those 200 are ONE budget for every client there is. Reproduced on a scratch Postgres: 200 finished rows plus one newly late client gave 200 candidates, none of them the live one, 0 messages. The 'has this rung been used?' question is now inside the SQL, so an unchaseable row cannot take a place; and a pass that fills its budget now reports budget_exhausted plus not_reached (null, never 0, if that count itself fails) with a warning line, because the tally reading 'considered 200 / queued 0 / skipped 200' is what hid it. (2) A LEGAL THREAT EXPIRED. The escalation check read the client's most recent 200 inbound messages; the portal chat writes one row per client turn, so 'my lawyer will be in touch' plus 210 ordinary rows put the threat outside the window and a text was queued to a permanently stopped client — reproduced. db/migrations/368 adds client_escalations: one row per client, written on FIRST sighting, never updated, never deleted (fundhub_app is granted no DELETE on it), and it outlives the message it was found in. The 200-row window is gone entirely; history is read once behind a watermark in client_escalation_scans that advances only over rows actually read, oldest first, after the scan finishes, so it can delay a stop and cannot cause a missed one. The database pre-filter is DERIVED from the same keyword list the code checks, widened not copied, and a test fails if it is ever narrower. The row stores which of OUR rules matched, never the client's words. (3) THE JOURNEY PAGE CLAIMED SOMETHING THE DATABASE DID NOT ENFORCE. It said no staff member, no workflow and no hand-written SQL could put a regulator complaint in state 'filed'. Both halves were disproved by running them: a direct INSERT landed straight on 'filed' because 366's forward-only trigger was BEFORE UPDATE OF state and an INSERT never fires it, and a hand-written UPDATE from sent to filed carrying filed_source='client_reported' was accepted. db/migrations/367 closes the INSERT. The second CANNOT be closed — filed_source='client_reported' IS the assertion that the client said so, and a database can refuse a row that lacks the assertion but cannot tell a true one from a false one. The page now carries an enforced/not-enforced table saying exactly that, and a test named KNOWN LIMIT pins the gap so closing it later is visible. The product rule is unchanged: rounds 4, 5 and 6 of the letter ladder still claim no filing, and a test in this lane fails if that changes. (4) ONE PERSON, TWO TEXTS. The daily cap was UNIQUE (client_id, client_local_date), which counts RECORDS — two client rows on one phone are two records, so that person got two texts in a day. Reproduced with '+15550004000' and '+1 (555) 000-4000': 2 outbound messages. db/migrations/369 adds a second cap on (org_id, destination_key, client_local_date) with the address normalised, KEPT ALONGSIDE the first rather than replacing it, because replacing it would newly allow one client an SMS and an email in one day — more messages, the wrong direction. Scoped to one company so one white-label partner's send cannot silence another's. The North American number rule is stated as the gap it is: a non-US number written two ways will not collide, which fails toward one extra message and never toward a missed stop. ALSO: 365's header described claim-then-queue while the code INSERTed outcome='queued' BEFORE sendTemplated, so a pass dying mid-send left a row reading exactly like a delivered nudge — reproduced by reading the row from inside the send callback. The code now writes 'claimed' and resolves it afterwards; a claimed row still holds the client's day, because not knowing whether a message went out is not a reason to send another. A missing template now names its key in the tally (template_pending_keys) instead of only a count — db/seed/025 still gives no templates to a company created after it ran, which is recorded as an open gap and not fixed here. The queued body is now pinned character for character, for the fixture copy AND for the shipped seed/025 copy, because the previous report quoted a string without generating it. Proved: 54 tests, 54 pass, 0 fail, 0 skipped, exit code 0, run TWICE against the same scratch Postgres 16.14. COMPLIANCE REVIEW REQUIRED: credit-repair messaging and dispute logic. | Reviewers found the chase silently dead for every client, a lawyer's warning ageing out of a 200-message window, and a page claiming a database rule that did not exist — all three reproduced by running the code. | branch fix/nudge-r2-2026-09-06
2026-09-06 | waypoint-nudge | THE OVERDUE CHECKLIST ROW NOW GETS CHASED, AND THE CHASE TERMINATES. Nothing in this platform reached out when a step the CLIENT owns went past its date — the progress page showed it and that was all. src/nudge/ adds the ladder: text on the due date, email at +2 days, text at +5, and at +9 a task for the CSM with NO client message. There is no fifth rung and there cannot be one — db/migrations/365_waypoint_nudges.sql carries CHECK (step BETWEEN 1 AND 4) and UNIQUE (waypoint_id, step), so the cap is stored rather than remembered by the scheduler. A second partial unique index on (client_id, client_local_date) caps every client at ONE client-facing message per day across ALL their checklist rows, measured in the CLIENT'S own timezone, so three late rows produce one text and not three. Both rows are written BEFORE anything is queued, which is the direct fix for 2026-09-03 when a chase loop sent 51 identical texts to one phone in two hours: the record IS the decision, so sixteen duplicate triggers collapse to one send — proved sequentially AND concurrently. Every exit condition is re-checked at SEND time against the live row, not once at schedule time: done or skipped, deleted or ours now, the paid alternative bought, rung 4 spent, the client replied, STOP or a lawyer, program complete or cancelled. A check that throws returns a STOP, never a neutral answer. No verified contact for a channel is a SKIP not a stop — the rung is spent so an impossible text is not retried forever and the ladder advances. Quiet hours are measured where the CLIENT is, layered on top of the dispatcher's Phoenix window, and a message outside their daytime is not queued at all rather than queued and held. NOTHING SENDS: sendTemplated writes one messages row status='queued' and src/messaging/dispatch.mjs sends it, and a test asserts there is no fetch and no provider import anywhere under src/nudge/. db/migrations/366_regulator_complaints.sql closes the absence src/metro2/letters/catalog.mjs:57-65 named: rounds 4 and 5 are prepared / sent / filed, and filed is unreachable without filed_source='client_reported' — staff cannot set it, no workflow sets it, and raw SQL is refused by a CHECK and a forward-only trigger. The first version of that CHECK used `=` and was therefore decorative: NULL = 'client_reported' is NULL, a CHECK only rejects on FALSE, so state='filed' with no source passed. IS NOT DISTINCT FROM closes it, and the raw-SQL test is what caught it. ON HOLD IS NOT IMPLEMENTED and was not invented — repair_programs.status has no such value and no table in this schema pauses a client; the lever that exists is setting a checklist row to 'blocked'. consent_sms is NOT required, matching every other send path in this repo, and that is recorded as a gap rather than papered over. Copy is placeholder on purpose (db/seed/025) behind three stable keys so Chris swaps the words without touching code. 2026-09-06 | dispute-rounds, repair-floor-flow | NO LETTER LEAVES THIS BUILDING ADDRESSED TO THE WORD "CLIENT", AND TWO SENTENCES IN YESTERDAY'S WRITE-UP WERE WRONG AND ARE CORRECTED. COMPLIANCE REVIEW REQUIRED — dispute logic and credit-repair messaging. ONE — THE NAME. Yesterday's entry said there were "exactly three places in this repository that print a customer's name onto a document". A reviewer found five, two of them outside the main source folder, and PROVED it by rendering: a Round 1 Experian letter came out headed `Client` and signed `Signature: ____  Client`. That is a letter a real person posts to a credit bureau about their own money. This time the list was read off the filesystem across all five folders and pasted, and instead of patching each place there is now ONE rule in one file that every writer loads — including the vendor writer, which is written in a different module style and is exactly why the last search could not see it. THE RULE: a person's name on a letter to a credit bureau, on a demand to a debt collector, or on a complaint sworn under penalty of perjury is either a real name or it is absent. A document that cannot be truthfully addressed REFUSES to be built. Nine sites now refuse or answer "unknown": the bureau letter, the signature line, the sworn declaration, the collector demand, both complaints, the whole do-it-yourself packet, the inquiry-removal draft, the funding/repair pack, and the vendor writer's three name sites — one of which was not a letterhead at all but the sentence "Please keep only my legal name: Client. Remove every other name." mailed to a bureau. Somebody genuinely called "Pat Client" still gets their letters: only a whole value counts as a stand-in. Proved by rendering every one of them, plus a check that fails if a new place ever appears. TWO — THE COMPLAINT FORMS ARE NOT DO-IT-YOURSELF ONLY. Yesterday's entry said the federal-regulator and state-attorney-general complaints "only come out of the do-it-yourself packet, not the repair desk". The opposite is true: that builder returns immediately for everything EXCEPT the repair desk. Corrected in the write-up and gated the same way as everything else. THREE — A PAGE SAID ROUND 6 NEVER CLAIMS A COMPLAINT WAS FILED. IT CAN. The page said "nothing in any round says a complaint HAS been filed"; a reviewer rendered Round 6 and got "On 2026-08-01 a complaint about this file was mailed to the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau". The page is what was wrong, and it is rewritten to the true, narrower claim, traced through the code: only Round 6 prints it, one sentence per recorded piece, and the record can only be written after the mail company has actually taken the letter AND handed back its own receipt number. No receipt, no record, and Round 6 says nothing — even when a complaint really did go out. Losing a true sentence is recoverable; mailing a false one to a credit bureau is not. FOUR — A TEST WAS QUIETLY PRINTING A BLANK NAME. Turning the rule on caught three letter tests that had been building letters headed "[Consumer Name]" for months and checking nothing about it. Fixture corrected, and one check was made stricter rather than looser: it now requires every opening sentence to exist word-for-word in the approved copy, which the old version could not tell apart from an invented one. | Yesterday's repair said the problem was fixed in three places; a reviewer found more, and printed a real dispute letter signed with the word "Client" to prove it. Now one rule covers every document that leaves the building, a page that wrongly promised we never claim a complaint was filed has been corrected, and nothing can say a complaint was filed unless the mail company gives us a receipt for it. | branch letters/r3-w8b-2026-09-06
2026-09-06 | dispute-rounds, repair-floor-flow | ROUND 3 STOPS TELLING A CREDIT BUREAU IT IS THE LAST LETTER, AND NO LETTER GOES OUT UNDER A NAME NOBODY CHECKED. COMPLIANCE REVIEW REQUIRED — dispute logic and credit-repair messaging. FOUR THINGS, every one of them reproduced by RUNNING the code before it was touched, and re-run after. ONE — ROUND 3 WAS MAILING A LIE. Four lines in the Round 3 wording call the letter the last one the bureau will get: "This is my last letter to your bureau on these items before I file with the CFPB and my state attorney general", "This Round 3 letter is the last bureau notice on these Metro 2 defects", and two more like them. That was TRUE while rounds 4, 5 and 6 produced nothing. The previous change is what made those rounds start sending, so it is what turned those four sentences into a false statement mailed to a credit bureau in a real person's name. The stripper that rewrites exactly these sentences already existed — and was switched on for rounds 4, 5 and 6 only, which is every round except the one where the claim had just become false. MEASURED BEFORE THE FIX, by rendering: 972 letters (six rounds x three bureaus x eighteen regeneration attempts x three claim mixes), of which 90 of the 162 Round 3 letters carried one of these lines. AFTER: zero, in Round 3 and in rounds 1, 2, 4 and 5. Round 6 is the last rung of the ladder, so Round 6 is the one letter allowed to say it is the last, and it says so in its own words — those 162 letters are byte-for-byte identical to what they were before, and so are rounds 4 and 5 and the furnisher letters. I ALSO WENT LOOKING FOR THE SAME CLASS ELSEWHERE, because a sample is not a proof: all 112 distinct sentences the letter writer can draw were listed out of the source across all seven rounds, and exactly 13 contain finality wording — 4 in Round 3 (all four fixed), 7 in Round 6 (all true), and 2 in Round 1 which say the letter is NOT a final notice, which is honest. Rounds 2, 4, 5 and the furnisher letter contain none. The honesty test that is supposed to catch this excluded Round 3 the same way the code did; it now checks every round but Round 6, against a wider family of phrases than the three it knew about. Proved by running: with the old gate put back, that test fails with 307 Round 3 hits; with the fix, it passes. TWO — A CORRECT DATE OF BIRTH WAS BEING REPORTED AS SOMEBODY ELSE'S. The credit file writes a birthday as 1985-03-02. A driving licence writes whatever was printed on it, commonly 02/03/1985. Same day, 2 March 1985, written two ways, and nothing on either side says which half is the month. The code picked month-first and fired claim M2-033, which tells a bureau that a wrong date of birth is one of the strongest signs another person's records have been merged into this file. An accusation, built out of a formatting difference. Run on that exact pair before the fix, it fired. Now an ambiguous date is UNKNOWN and makes no claim: every day the string could mean is listed, and the claim is made only when no reading of one side can be the same day as any reading of the other. A birthday that is wrong however you read it still fires — proved on three separate mismatched pairs — and a date where one half cannot be a month, like 03/22/1985, was never ambiguous and is read normally. This only started mattering because the previous change is what first connected the customer's own documents to this check. THREE — A SENTENCE IN THE LAST WRITE-UP DID NOT MATCH THE CODE, AND THE CODE WAS THE WRONG HALF. It said a repair customer whose ID has not been read "now gets NO letter at all". What the code did was refuse only when there was nothing else to say. A customer with an unread ID and one real collection on the file still got all three letters, with just the name and address requests missing. Chris was asked to make a decision on the version that did not exist. The refusal is the half that was kept, and here is why — see FOUR. FOUR — THE NAME ON THE LETTER WAS STILL A TYPED FORM FIELD. Only the name and address REQUESTS inside the letter were held to the uploaded ID. The name printed at the top of the letter, and signed at the bottom, still came from what a closer typed into the customer record — which the same file's own note calls "what somebody typed into a form ... Neither is evidence". So a letter could go to a credit bureau addressed and signed in a name no document had ever proved. Proved by running it: with a customer record saying "Sim Repair" and an ID saying "Simone Repair-Vega", every letter said Sim Repair, twice. A letter that cannot be addressed truthfully refuses rather than guesses, so both halves now stand on the document: the ID check runs BEFORE any letter is built and stops all of them, however bad the file is, and the printed name comes off the ID. It is gated on the NAME and not on both documents, because the name is what a bureau matches a file by — a customer with a verified name and no accepted proof of address still gets letters, with no address claim in them. The reply address on the envelope is unchanged and may still fall back to the customer's company address; that is where to post the answer, not a claim about where anybody lives. NOT DONE, and recorded rather than papered over: there are exactly three places in this repository that print a customer's name onto a document, and they were listed off the filesystem rather than from memory — the bureau letters, the furnisher letters, and the CFPB / state attorney general complaints. The first two are reached from the repair desk and both are now gated. The third is only reached from the do-it-yourself packet, which resolves the name through a different module that reads the typed customer record and falls back to the literal word "Client". That path was not touched and is not covered by any of the above. | Round 3 letters were telling credit bureaus they were the last letter anyone would send, which stopped being true the day rounds 4, 5 and 6 started going out; a customer's own correct birthday was being reported to a bureau as evidence of a mixed file; and the name on every letter came from a form field instead of the ID we made the customer upload. | branch letters/r2-w8b-p2-2026-09-06
2026-09-06 | role-inquiry-remover, repair-floor-flow, dispute-rounds | THE LETTER NO LONGER ARGUES WITH ITSELF, AND THE NAME ON IT COMES FROM A DOCUMENT AGAIN. Three things, all measured by rendering real letters against a scratch Postgres, not by reading code. ONE — THE MIXED LETTER. The ordinary repair customer has real problems on their credit file AND personal information that is already correct, so one envelope holds both: "this collection is wrong, delete it" beside "my name is right, hold my file to it". Every sentence around those items still spoke as if the whole list were wrong — "delete each item you cannot verify", "the items below are reported inaccurately", "describe the method of verification for each item and give me each furnisher's name". Read as written, a bureau was being asked to delete the customer's own correctly reported name, and there is no furnisher of a person's own name. MEASURED before the fix: 972 letters built (three claim mixes x six rounds x three bureaus x eighteen regeneration attempts), 855 sentences across them, 55 of them distinct, said something the confirmations in that same letter did not support; rounds 2, 3, 5 and 6 had no mixed wording written for them at all and rounds 1 and 4 were half covered. Every one of those sentences now narrows itself to "the disputed items" and asks for the confirmations separately. AFTER: 216 hits, 12 distinct, and all twelve are from the two families that are deliberately left alone — "remove what you cannot verify" (a confirmation claim asks for exactly that, so it is true of both kinds of claim) and "tell me what came off and what stayed" (a request for a report, not a claim about an item). TWO — THE IDENTITY MODULE WAS NEVER FOUND. `src/repair/analyze.mjs` looked for the module that holds what a customer's government ID actually proved at three paths — index.mjs, verified-identity.mjs, identity.mjs — and it had landed at `src/identity/verified.mjs`. All three imports threw, the answer resolved to null, and null means unknown, so on EVERY real customer the name claim and the address claim were dropped and three engine rules (name variants, date of birth, employment) stayed dark. The module was built and exporting exactly the function wanted. Nothing reached it. It survived because every test injected the module by hand, so the seam itself was never once exercised; there is now a test that uses no override and fails if the file moves again. THREE — A REFUSAL THAT WAS TELLING STAFF THE WRONG THING. A repair customer whose ID has not been read yet gets no letter, because a mailed dispute may not name a person on the strength of a field a closer typed. That is correct and it stays. What it answered was `no_violations`, which the Repair desk prints as "the credit file looks clean — nothing to dispute" — the file is not the problem, the missing document is, and a Specialist reading that closes the case. New reason `identity_not_verified`, wired through api/repair/generate.mjs and the desk's existing reason map, saying which document to go and get. Customers NOT on the repair path still get plain `no_violations`, pinned by a test. THE PROOF, and it is the whole point of this entry: 48 bureau letters rendered end to end through the real analyzeAndGenerate against a scratch Postgres and read one by one — a spotless file over rounds 1 to 6 (18 letters), a mixed file with two collections and two confirmations over rounds 1 to 6 (18), a damaged file rounds 1 and 2 (6), a repair customer with no verified address (3), and the same damaged file on a customer NOT on the repair path (3). Always all three bureaus, never one letter shown as "the letter". ZERO of the 48 carry a sentence their own claims do not support. The customer with no verified address gets a name claim and NO address claim anywhere in any of the three letters — the letterhead still prints a reply address, which is the envelope's job and not a statement about where anybody lives. The customer off the repair path gets engine findings only: zero PI-*, zero DEROG-*. Rounds 4, 5 and 6 name the CFPB and the state attorney general only in the future tense; nothing in this repository records whether either complaint was ever filed, so no letter says one was. ALSO FIXED, and it was a mistake made in this session: merging PR 339 into PR 341 hit a conflict in analyze.mjs and it was resolved to 339's side wholesale, which silently dropped main's fix for letters stuck on 'sending' or 'delivered'. Two tests that were green on main went red. Main's fix is restored with its comment. FOLDED IN: PR 339 (feat/letters-all-rounds) is merged into this branch and can be closed rather than left dangling — both pull requests edited src/metro2/letters/ and would have collided. NOT DONE, recorded rather than papered over: the letter template prints its `lead` sentence AFTER the itemised claims in one of its three layouts, so a line reading "the items below" sits above them. It is a layout quirk that predates all of this, it makes no false claim about anybody's credit file, and changing paragraph order would change every letter's variance fingerprint. 2026-09-03 | role-inquiry-remover, repair-floor-flow (new) | A CREDIT-REPAIR CUSTOMER WHOSE FILE IS SPOTLESS NOW GETS A LETTER TOO, WITH TWO NAMED EXCEPTIONS THAT ARE IN THIS ENTRY AND NOT BURIED: a customer on Round 2 or later whose newest credit pull is older than the previous round's letters gets NOTHING until a fresh pull lands (credit_file_stale_for_round, below), and a customer with no home address on record gets the name cleanup with NO address claim, because a letter may not state an address nobody has given us. Owner-set, final: on every customer on the repair path, on every round, clean file or not, always run personal-information cleanup — one name, one address, and a dispute of every inquiry with no matching account. Before this, `src/repair/analyze.mjs` walked a tidy file straight into `no_violations` and the Repair desk stayed empty for somebody who had paid: the Metro 2 engine only fires on a reporting defect and the derogatory pass only fires on a derogatory account, so a customer with neither got nothing at all. MEASURED BEFORE THE FIX, on a scratch Postgres with the real endpoint: a repair customer with one name, one address, one clean account and one explained inquiry produced zero dispute_letters rows. MEASURED AFTER: one row, rule ids PI-NAME-CONFIRM + PI-ADDRESS-CONFIRM. The new module is `src/metro2/diy/personal-info-floor.mjs` and the identity read in analyze.mjs is hoisted above the no_violations wall so the floor can name the customer's own name and address. THE RULE THAT MAKES IT SAFE, and it is the reason an earlier attempt at this was rejected: A CONSOLIDATION CLAIM IS ONLY MADE WHEN THE FILE GENUINELY CARRIES TWO OR MORE DIFFERENT NAMES OR ADDRESSES, and every name it quotes as being on the file is read off the file itself. The earlier attempt compared the bureau file against clients.first_name + clients.last_name, which never carries a middle name while a real bureau file routinely does, so on a file carrying the single name "Barbara M Doty" it generated a dispute asserting the file reported more than one name and demanding a bureau delete the customer's own correctly reported name — a false statement of fact in a mailed dispute. A file carrying exactly one name now gets PI-NAME-CONFIRM, which quotes that one name and asks the bureau to hold the file to it; a file showing no name at all gets a claim that asserts nothing about what the file contains. The name to keep comes from the customer record — the customer speaking about themselves — and is never used to decide what the bureau reported. The inquiry claim (PI-INQUIRY-UNMATCHED) never says the customer failed to authorise the inquiry, because nothing in a credit report carries that (src/metro2/normalize.mjs marks consumerAuthorized not_visible); it states only that no account from that company is reported anywhere on the pull, and asks for the permissible purpose or deletion. Inquiry matching is deliberately generous and spans all three bureaus: an uncertain match counts as a match and no claim is made, because under-firing costs a dispute while over-firing puts a false statement in a mailed letter. THE FLOOR IS GATED ON THE REPAIR PATH exactly as the derogatory pass already was — a customer off that path gets what they always got, engine findings or nothing. PI-* joins the variance gate's claim-block list in src/metro2/letters/variance.mjs because the floor says the same words at all three bureaus by definition. CORRECTED 2026-09-03 — the reason first written here was tested by a verifier and was WRONG. It said a clean file would otherwise have produced one letter and two variance_gate_exhausted refusals at Round 1. It would not: prior letters are compared per bureau, so the three Round 1 letters are never compared against each other and a clean Round 1 file produced three letters either way. What the change actually buys is ROUND 2 AND LATER, where the new letter IS compared against the same bureau's Round 1 letter. ROUNDS: Round 2 and later now refuse with credit_file_stale_for_round when the newest pull is older than the previous round's letters — a refusal, not a lock, cleared by one fresh pull; R1 and the furnisher letters are exempt. Dropping items the bureaus already removed needs no code: every claim is computed from the newest stored pull. SIM DATA (row 13): every simulated bureau file carried empty name, address and social lists, so a walkthrough could not exercise any of this. The two repair-path profiles in scripts/sim/push-credit.mjs now carry three reported names built from the customer's own name (as recorded, a middle initial, a misspelled surname), a current and a prior address, a social beginning 000 — an area number never issued, so it cannot be a real person's — and inquiries from companies with no account on the file. funding, blueprint and academy are untouched and a test pins that by value. PROVED on a scratch Postgres with the real analyzeAndGenerate: clean repair file -> 1 letter (PI-NAME-CONFIRM, PI-ADDRESS-CONFIRM); the sim repair profile -> 5 letters, the three bureau letters each carrying M2-031 + DEROG-LATE + DEROG-COLLECTION + DEROG-CHARGEOFF/second DEROG-COLLECTION + PI-NAME-CONSOLIDATE + PI-ADDRESS-CONSOLIDATE + PI-INQUIRY-UNMATCHED, plus two furnisher letters; THE SAME DAMAGED FILE on a customer NOT on the repair path -> M2-031 only, zero PI-*, zero DEROG-*. NOT REGENERATED, and correct that it was not: `npm run journeys:check` says docs/journeys is up to date — no route moved and no route's principal gate moved. What changed is what one endpoint answers with, which that generator does not model. REPAIRED 2026-09-03, SECOND PASS, after two independent checkers read the finished LETTER rather than the claim list. FIRST: on a spotless file every claim in the letter said the file was CORRECT while the letter around them still told the bureau the file was inaccurate and demanded correction — "these two things are right, please fix them". A letter whose every claim is a confirmation is now headed a personal information confirmation, each claim is headed Request rather than Violation, and the sentences that stated the file was inaccurate or demanded deletion are swapped for sentences that ask for the confirmation. It reuses the substitution table the writer already had for letters with no Metro 2 claim in them; a letter with even one real dispute in it keeps the dispute wording. SECOND: a customer with no home address on record had their BUSINESS street address asserted as their home — "My address is 204 Horse Blvd" — and their real current residential address put in the delete list. The letterhead may still fall back to the company address, because the envelope needs a reply address; nothing inside the letter does. No address on record now means no address claim at all. Unknown stays unknown. Also fixed: the client's own address no longer lands in a delete list over a ZIP+4 or a spelled-out street type, a CONFIRM claim no longer asks the bureau to hold the file to a name or address the customer has not claimed as theirs, and the raw data blob that was being printed inside the letter is now written as plain phrases. 2026-09-06 | waypoint-nudge | ROUND FOUR. THE LINE BELOW THIS ONE GOT THE HARD PART RIGHT AND MISCLASSIFIED ONE THING, AND THAT ONE THING KEPT THE CHASE DEAD FOR THE WHOLE PLATFORM. (1) AN ABANDONED CHECKOUT LINK HELD A PLACE FOR EVER. A client sent a link to pay was held out of the chase queue because "a checkout link is out. It expires; then we chase again." NOTHING IN THIS SYSTEM EVER EXPIRED ONE. Enumerated 2026-09-06: no expiry date on the record, nothing set one when the link was made, and no scheduled job anywhere so much as named that table. The only thing that ever moved one of those records on was the payment webhook, and the journey page already recorded that the payment handler is not on the live bus. So the hold was permanent while being written down as temporary, it sorted first because it was oldest, and the sweeper searches every company at once, so those 200 places are one budget for every client there is. Both reviewers' measurement reproduced here before the fix, on a scratch Postgres 16.14: 200 clients each holding an unpaid checkout minted 400 days ago against a checklist row 400 days late, plus one newly late live client -> candidates 200, the live one not among them, 0 messages to them, budget full, and the same a year later; 0 ladder rows ever written for those 200, which is why round two's anti-join could not see them. FIXED BY GIVING THE INVITATION AN END DATE AND PUTTING IT IN THE DATA: SEVEN DAYS. The number lives in one file (src/paid-services/link-ttl.mjs), it is stamped on the record when the link is made, db/migrations/370 REFUSES a record sitting in 'waiting for payment' without one, an hourly job (src/workflows/paid-checkout-expiry-sweeper.mjs) closes the record to 'cancelled' with reason 'checkout_expired' when the date passes, and the chase search holds a place open only while the link is genuinely live. Seven and not thirty because the whole ladder is nine days long; a longer hold would swallow it. NO MONEY MOVES — a hosted link is an invitation and nothing has been charged, so closing one takes nothing from anybody and creates no refund, and the sweep refuses to touch any record that carries a payment amount or a payment time. Proved at 200 days and at 400 days: the 200 abandoned records are real work again, one pass hands each of them to a person and finishes their ladder for good, and THE LIVE CLIENT GETS THEIR MESSAGE ON THE VERY NEXT PASS — one hour, rather than never. (2) THE PERMANENCE PROMISE WAS STILL NOT TRUE. Round three's take-away of delete and edit rights was real and both reviewers confirmed it, but 368 also said the escalation record is swept away automatically when the CLIENT record is deleted, and that sweep runs with the table owner's permission rather than the application's. The app holds delete on clients. So the app could still destroy the record, by deleting the client, while two more places said flatly "the application really cannot delete one". db/migrations/370 changes that link so the database REFUSES to delete a client who has one of these records on file. Proved as fundhub_app (not a superuser, does not bypass row-level security): the delete is rejected outright, error 23503, the record is still there afterwards, and a client with no such record still deletes normally. (3) A SENTENCE ABOUT SOMEBODY ELSE ENDED EVERY CHASE, FOR EVER, INVISIBLY. "that collection agency that keeps calling me is a scam" is a client complaining about a third party — the exact case the word list says it is trying to avoid — and it wrote the permanent record and ended every chase that client will ever have. Round three's take-away means no part of the app can undo it and nothing outside the chase code reads that table, so NO SCREEN SHOWED IT HAD HAPPENED. The stop stays permanent and the word list was NOT narrowed — every narrowing tried also let through language plainly aimed at us. What is fixed is the silence: the first time a client is stopped this way a task lands on the CSM's board naming OUR rule, never the client's sentence, once per client ever. STILL OPEN AND SAID SO: there is no way to lift one from inside the product, deliberately, so a misfire needs somebody with database access. (4) A REFUND MEANT WE DID NOT DO THE WORK AND WE STOPPED ASKING ANYWAY. 'refunded' counted as "they paid us to handle this one", so a client whose money went back was never chased again about a job that is theirs again. 2026-09-06 | waypoint-nudge | ROUND THREE. THE LINE BELOW THIS ONE OVERCLAIMED AND THIS ONE CORRECTS IT — two of the four things it said were fixed were not. (1) THE CHASE STILL DIED FOR EVERYONE, AND THE ROUND-TWO FIX WAS AIMED AT THE WRONG CASE. The pass takes the 200 oldest late rows and only then asks, one at a time, whether each may be chased. Round two excluded a row whose current rung had ALREADY BEEN USED. The case that was actually failing writes no row at all: a client who said STOP, mentioned a lawyer, finished or cancelled their program, paid us to do it, or already replied is refused AFTER the 200 are chosen, nothing is written, so the row stays late, stays oldest, sorts FIRST and holds a place for ever. The sweeper searches every company at once, so those 200 places are one budget for the whole platform. Both reviewers reproduced it independently and both were reproduced again here before the fix: 200 cancelled programs plus one newly late client gave 200 candidates, the live one not among them, 0 messages, same again the next day; 200 clients who had texted STOP gave exactly the same. FIXED by moving every PERMANENT reason to stop INSIDE the query, before the limit — opted out, a lawyer, program complete or cancelled, paid the alternative, already replied, ladder finished, the row blocked, done, or ours rather than theirs. TEMPORARY reasons are deliberately left in the queue and keep their place, because tomorrow they can send: a checkout link still out, the one-message-a-day cap, night in the client's timezone, or a database error we could not read. This is a SHORTER queue and never a looser one — every clause mirrors a check the send-time gate was already making a moment later, the gate still runs on every message, and a test fails if the two ever disagree. Proved with both reviewers' scenarios and with a temporarily held row that keeps its place and is reached the day the hold lifts. One honest gap: a client who has threatened us but has never been scanned still holds a place for exactly ONE pass — the pass that scans them, refuses them and writes the permanent record; nothing is sent to them on it. (2) THE PERMANENCE PROMISE WAS FALSE, IN FIVE PLACES INCLUDING THE LINE BELOW. It said the application cannot delete a legal-escalation record. It could. db/migrations/104 runs ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES granting SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on every table made after it, so 368's GRANT SELECT, INSERT added nothing and removed nothing; confirmed on a migrated database, DELETE privilege = true. 368 now carries an explicit REVOKE of UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE — the shape the sister lane proved in 361 and 363 — and it is proved as fundhub_app (not a superuser, does not bypass row-level security) on a database built from zero: DELETE and UPDATE both raise permission denied, error 42501, and the row is still there afterwards; INSERT and SELECT still work, which the detector needs. A silent 'DELETE 0' would not have been a refusal. src/nudge/escalation-permanence.pg.test.mjs fails if any of that is ever undone. (3) THE LEGAL THREAT STILL EXPIRED. The line below says the read watermark advanced only over rows the pre-filter returned. The code advanced it to max(created_at) over ALL inbound rows and the next pass read with a strict 'created_at > mark', so a threat landing exactly ON the mark was invisible for ever. Reproduced here: scan 1 false, the lawyer row present in messages, scan 2 false, blockersFor [], deliverNudge queued, 1 message to the lawyer-threat client. A '>=' boundary would close that one shape and leave a backfilled, imported or clock-skewed message behind the mark permanently invisible, and `messages` has no insertion-ordered column to mark instead. SO THE WATERMARK IS GONE, table and all: the whole inbound history is read every pass until the permanent record exists. The cost was measured, not assumed — 3.5 ms at 500 inbound rows, 8.4 ms at 2,000, 39.9 ms at 10,000, and only ever for a client who has NOT threatened us. (4) THE JOURNEY PAGE DESCRIBED A SCREEN THAT DOES NOT EXIST. It said a page renders 'sent' as sent and 'filed' as filed alongside who said so. Nothing outside src/nudge/ reads regulator_complaints and src/nudge/regulator.mjs has no production caller — no endpoint, no route, no workflow. The page now says UNVERIFIED and says why. THE PRODUCT RULES ARE UNCHANGED: rounds 4 and 5 still claim no filing, no client-facing copy was touched, and 'credit repair' still appears in none of it. MEASURED, exit code read on every run: src/nudge/run.pg.test.mjs 42 tests 42 pass 0 fail 0 skipped exit 0, twice against the same scratch Postgres 16.14 (Homebrew, macOS); regulator.pg.test.mjs 17/17 exit 0 twice; escalation-permanence.pg.test.mjs 3/3 exit 0 twice. The sweeper searches every company at once, so those 200 are ONE budget for every client there is. Reproduced on a scratch Postgres: 200 finished rows plus one newly late client gave 200 candidates, none of them the live one, 0 messages. The 'has this rung been used?' question is now inside the SQL, so an unchaseable row cannot take a place; and a pass that fills its budget now reports budget_exhausted plus not_reached (null, never 0, if that count itself fails) with a warning line, because the tally reading 'considered 200 / queued 0 / skipped 200' is what hid it. (2) A LEGAL THREAT EXPIRED. The escalation check read the client's most recent 200 inbound messages; the portal chat writes one row per client turn, so 'my lawyer will be in touch' plus 210 ordinary rows put the threat outside the window and a text was queued to a permanently stopped client — reproduced. db/migrations/368 adds client_escalations: one row per client, written on FIRST sighting, never updated, never deleted (fundhub_app is granted no DELETE on it), and it outlives the message it was found in. The 200-row window is gone entirely; history is read once behind a watermark in client_escalation_scans that advances only over rows actually read, oldest first, after the scan finishes, so it can delay a stop and cannot cause a missed one. The database pre-filter is DERIVED from the same keyword list the code checks, widened not copied, and a test fails if it is ever narrower. The row stores which of OUR rules matched, never the client's words. (3) THE JOURNEY PAGE CLAIMED SOMETHING THE DATABASE DID NOT ENFORCE. It said no staff member, no workflow and no hand-written SQL could put a regulator complaint in state 'filed'. Both halves were disproved by running them: a direct INSERT landed straight on 'filed' because 366's forward-only trigger was BEFORE UPDATE OF state and an INSERT never fires it, and a hand-written UPDATE from sent to filed carrying filed_source='client_reported' was accepted. db/migrations/367 closes the INSERT. The second CANNOT be closed — filed_source='client_reported' IS the assertion that the client said so, and a database can refuse a row that lacks the assertion but cannot tell a true one from a false one. The page now carries an enforced/not-enforced table saying exactly that, and a test named KNOWN LIMIT pins the gap so closing it later is visible. The product rule is unchanged: rounds 4, 5 and 6 of the letter ladder still claim no filing, and a test in this lane fails if that changes. (4) ONE PERSON, TWO TEXTS. The daily cap was UNIQUE (client_id, client_local_date), which counts RECORDS — two client rows on one phone are two records, so that person got two texts in a day. Reproduced with '+15550004000' and '+1 (555) 000-4000': 2 outbound messages. db/migrations/369 adds a second cap on (org_id, destination_key, client_local_date) with the address normalised, KEPT ALONGSIDE the first rather than replacing it, because replacing it would newly allow one client an SMS and an email in one day — more messages, the wrong direction. Scoped to one company so one white-label partner's send cannot silence another's. The North American number rule is stated as the gap it is: a non-US number written two ways will not collide, which fails toward one extra message and never toward a missed stop. ALSO: 365's header described claim-then-queue while the code INSERTed outcome='queued' BEFORE sendTemplated, so a pass dying mid-send left a row reading exactly like a delivered nudge — reproduced by reading the row from inside the send callback. The code now writes 'claimed' and resolves it afterwards; a claimed row still holds the client's day, because not knowing whether a message went out is not a reason to send another. A missing template now names its key in the tally (template_pending_keys) instead of only a count — db/seed/025 still gives no templates to a company created after it ran, which is recorded as an open gap and not fixed here. The queued body is now pinned character for character, for the fixture copy AND for the shipped seed/025 copy, because the previous report quoted a string without generating it.
